PRACTICAL POULTRY FARMING.

(By Redcap.)

FITTEXING EARLY clircu,s,

All the early chickens intended for culinary pur- poem should at once be got together and fattened off. I do not suppose that any of my readers are in so large a way that they need adopt the cramming syatem. It is chiefly a mixed business with email poultry- keeping in our district. Therefore I should recom- mend the old method of fattening. Gather all the young cockerels together, let them occupy a shed with but a small run—which, by-the-way, ahotild be kept very slean—and feed not less than three time. per day. You should give only sufficient food to well satisfy them at each meal. Under no con- sideration let any fond be laft to go sour iu the pens ; because if no the birds will soon get sickened and this will put them back con- aiderably. Beans and peas (ground), oatmeal and middlings are all great flesh former., and should be used for fattening purpoees. I am not an advocate of maize meal, it produces too much internal fat or oil and it is flesh you want not fat. Cover the hones well. Kept under proper conditions and feed on a mixture of the above meals (with an occasional feed of wheat) they should be ready for killing is abut four or five weeks. If you can get a supply of Aim milk—wherewith to mix the food—so much the better.

.. Ilisf.,re being killed, the birds should he left without- food for about twenty-four hours. When this process is adopted they dress much better, and there is not the same tendancy to decomposition. During this petiod the fowl does not lose in weight as is sometimes supposed. The fowl instead falls back On its reserves, which com- prise the internal fatty parts, and the loss in weight is practically nothing.

===== 0002601_19110610_art0009.txt | included=true | included =====
.„ ..... The best and most huniane way to kill a fowl ruby dielocating the neck. Take the bird in the left hand by the legs. and grasp the bead with the right hand ; get the head between the first and second finger ; having than far proceeded, let the left hand, rest on the left thigh, and then with a steady down- ward and outward stretch dislocate the vertebral column where it is connected with the bead. As noon an this is done no pain is felt by the fowl because it must be absolutely unconscious. Kee,' the bird head downwards while the muscles are convulsing so that all tire blood !nay drain into the head then hang the bird up by the legs. If you have a large number to kill, do not kill more than six at once, but proceed to pluck.

..„ ... should be done before the body is cold, the main object being to get out the feathers without tearing the skin. There is no hard and fast rule on plucking; no two .persons adopt quite the same method. Horn- eves it is advisable first of all to remove the large wing and tail feathers, then proceed with the neck and pluck r.ff all that remains. The parte which are most easily torn are the feather tracts on either side of the breast, and until you become efficient you should he content to enclose one or two feathers at a time. A badly plucked fowl does uot look appetising when I laced on the market Mall. If there are a minder of torn places which always go lunch), it makes the fowl look dirty. therefore it is essential that the greatest care should be taken when plucking. The best way to remove the stelibs (commonly called pens) is by holding the fowl with its head toward you, then pass the blade of as old dull knife under each stub') and draw out. You i should, by-the-way, try and avoid killing stock that base a lot of these immature feathers on them. In the case of dark-feathered fowls it is most difficult indeed to remove all. So yon trill agree that it is I much better to avoid killing while in this state. In the case of white-feathered fowls it does not matter much as the stubbe do not show up the name as in tiark-coloured varieties.

..... ~„.. After plucking and stubbing they require singeing. You will notice that the Indy is covered all over with fine silky hours These must be removed. Make a blaze of lighted paper or straw and hold the bird, with the head in one hand and the legs with toe other. then pass it though the flame several times. Be careful not to let the body come into contact with the smoke for it will soon blacken the skin. The burnt hairs are easily dusted off by a stoke with the hand. After singeing remove all stains and dirt from the beak, feet and vent. By holding the feet and legs in boiling water for a few toinutes the dirt and objectionable scales on the legs are easily removed.

===== 0002601_19110610_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
TIN

We now proceed to the finishing touch, that of trussing. It is most difficult to describe this process by writing. Practical demonstrations are rtmch better, and by such much more easily learned. How- ever we will do as well as possible seeing that we have no other form of communication. The points of the winge should first be twisted over the back, the fowl should be placed breast upwards with the fret towards you, The legs should then I. forced for- ward and downward until that upon the table. Then insert a skewer above the first joint of the wing, pressed through the leg just below the body joint over the back. Take a piece of clean this twine and secure it at each end of the skewer so that it may ho!.1 the whole in poaition. Clean the gizzard and place under one wing with the liver under the coher wing. The vent which you made to empty the carcase must be covered by bringing the skin over the"par.on's not.," and securely tying with the fine twine. If a fowl is dressed neatly it need not be washed. As a matter of fact it should not be washed, because in doing so much of the flavour is lost. Some people give them a dust over with the flower dredger, which it. a bad practice. If a fowl has inadvettently been torn, the flour turns black in these placeP, and snake, it look most unsightly. Old hens and cocks which are sold f..r boiling can be dressed in the same way. But they should have the head and legs removed. By this procees you coo easily denote the roasters from the boilers. Keep faith with 'your customers ; never deceive them by selling an' old us' foes' young an' for by sr doing you soon lose your trade. Remember the old adsge : " Once bit twice shy." Beside., theme is no reason why you should take spite out of the property owner by knocking plaster off his call in an attempt t., separate the Ilesli from the bone of

INWITRY IN RESTIIICTEIN AREAS.

When fowls are confined continually to one plot of ground the danger of contaminating the e,nl is large!y overcome by something being grcern in the run. When the run is laid don in grass it is necessary that it should be regularly and frequently mown, , otherwise the rain does not can y the manure into the , soil, lint lets it remain inn poisonous state among the long coarse grans. Of all methods that have been adopted by the poultry keeper, whose space is restricted, there is none so sound in practice as the division of the run. The advice to reduce die number of fowls is sound sod easily given, hut it is rarely adopted, as the temptation to keep a larger number than the amount of ground will safely carry is too great for the majority of small poultry keepers to resist. A division of the space, therefore, is strongly recommended, efteh section being used alternately. This goes far to overcome the danger .1 overcrowding.

FROM LABOUR'S STANDPOINT.

By Philip Snowden, M.P.

THE OSBORNE BILL.

The Bill for dealing with the situation ,created by the decision of the House of Lords in the Osborne case has been read a second time in the House of Commons without a division. Such apparent unanimity may be misleading. It was, paradoxical as it may seem, because the Bill pleased nobody that it was allowed to go to a Committee without any formal opposition. The Labour Party I are not satisfied with it in a good inany particulars. The Unionists seem to be of the opinion that the Hill gives far too much power to Trade Unions, and that the safe- guards it professes to erect for the protection of the minority are of very little practical value.

• The Government have certainly not con- ceded as much as the unions had been led to expect from the statements which had been made by the Prime Minister and others previous to the last election. The Bill appears to have been drafted with far more regard to the feelings of the dissentient minority than of those of the majority. The impression which is left on the mind of one who reads the Bill is that the Government were obliged to do something, and that they have done as little as they could, and that with very little relish. The Labour Party voted for the Second Reading of the Bill with the reservation that they were com- mitted only to the general principle of the Bill, and that they would endeavour to have it very drastically amended in Committee. The terms and scope of the amendments which the Labour Party are to move have not yet been placed upon the papers of the House, but if there is any intention to make the Bill conform to the unanimous and of t- repeated demands of Trades Union Congresses the amendments will have to be of a very drastic nature. The Bill does not attempt to restore the position as it was understood and acted upon before the House of Lords decision. It does give a Trade Union the freedom to take up political work under certain conditions, which are very irksome and cumbersome. The trade union demand has been for full liberty to make political work as much a regular part of its activities as the industrial side of its work. The unions have demanded the right to use , the funds of the union for political purposes,l or to make a compulsory levy tor that object. These demands are not met in the Bill now before Parliament.

===== 0002601_19110610_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
AN INDEFENSIBLE POSITION.

According to the Attorney General's state- ment of the scope of the Bill, it is intended to include a certain group of activities, which are on the border line between industrial and political questions, within the statutory objects of a trade union. These border line questions are such as attending a Trades Union Congress, the sending of delegates to interview Ministers and members of Parlia- ment about Labour legislation, and the ordinary work of trades councils. It is under- stood that the cost of carrying out such activities as these will be legally a charge upon the ordinary funds of the union, and no "conscientious objectors" will be allowed to contract out of paying for this expenditure. The Attorney General admitted that it would pass the wit of man to lay down a clean line of demarcation between the work of the trade unions which was political and that which was industrial. He has tried to do it in this Bill, and if it passes in its present form it is perfectly certain that the law courts will be provided with a good deal of work, and the lawyers with a good many fat fees, in trying to decide whether a trade union is entitled under this Bill to do certain things..

The Attorney General's confession that it is impossible to lay down a fixed line between what is the industrial work of a trade union and what is political work concedes the whole case for the complete reversal of the Osborne judgment. The industrial work of the unions is overflowing into the political field, and it will continue to do more and more. That is the justification for demand- ing that every member of a trade union who enjoys the benefits of the political work of the Labour movement shall pay for it. If the minority man who objects to paying for Labour representation is to be let off scot free, then there is a grievance estab- lished of which the majority man is the victim. The Attorney General gave an illustration which shattered all the arguments for the exemption of the minority mm. He pointed out that it was a common practice for trade unions to send deputations to the heads of the Government departments to endeavour to get grievances redressed. He cited the case of the railway workers, who might go to the President of the Board of Trade to get automatic coupling compulsorily put to every waggon. It is an industrial matter to get any reform which does not require legislation, but to get the same thing by legislation is political. In the first case every member of the union must be compelled under this Bill to pay, but in the second case he is to be exempted if he signs a declaration that he has a conscientious objection to mixing trade unions up in politics. TWO DEFINITIONS OF THE ONE THING.

This is the nonsensical position in which the Government have got themselves by trying to draw an impossible distinction. There is no line of demarcation now between things industrial and things political as they affect trade unions. The things which once could be settled by arrangement with employers have now to be settled by legisla- tion. The regulation of hours by arrange- ments between the workmen and the employers is ene of the oldest functions of a trade union. Every man who has joined a trade union in the last fifty years has joined with the full con- sciousness that that was one of the main objects of the union. But it has been dis- covered that it is better and more effective to get shorter hours by legislation than by negotiations. Under this Bill it is a statutory object of a trade union to negotiate about hours with the masters, and for this every member must pay ; but under the same Bill it is not a statutory object to send the miners' representatives into Parliament to get shorter hours. The whole case for the Bill as it stands is indefensible. It is not ecognising the actual state of things ; it is regislating for a by-gone condition. The Bill proposes that a trade union shall not be allowed to engage in political work unless it has had its rules on this matter approved by the Registrar. This applies to a non-registered union as well. It must have its rules about political work approved by the Registrar. though it does not appear that it must become a registered trade union he rules in regard to" political objects " are forth in full in the Bill. They require ballot of the members of the union

shall be taken, and that the decision shall rest with a majority of those voting. If the result of the ballot is in favour of political action, then this resolution must be conveyed to every member of the union, and at the same time he must be given a form on which he can claim exemption from payment and he must be informed that he need not pay unless he wishes to do so. That is what one may call the path of the conscientious

objector made easy. Surely there was no need to give this direct encouragement to the minority to contract out of paying for the benefits they are to share equally with the majority of the members of the union.

TRANSFERRING THE GRIEVANCE. The Bill is very unsatisfactory, as it stands. It was not to be expected that the Government would concede all the trade unions had demanded, but they might at least have not shown such a strong partiality for the minority man. But after all that is not to be wondered at. The minority man is either a Liberal or a Unionist, and he is therefore entitled to special consideration from the party to which he is determined to stick, even at the cost of dissociating himself from his own comrades in the trade union ranks. The grievance which will remain on the part of the majority of the members of the union if this Bill passes in anything like its present form will be, perhaps, more a sentimental than a financial one. The members who pay for Labolr representation for those who refuse to do so but are ready to take its benefits, will not be ruined by the strain. But men have always been ready to fight harder and to suffer more for senti- mental and conscientious grievances for the material ones. The grievance which the minority man had before the Osborne decision was only a sentimental one. Now it is to be transferred from the minority to the majority.

PEACE AND WAR.

THE GREAT ILLUSION.

No. 11. Sy C. E. Parker.

; In your last week's issue I called attention to the main argument in the first section of Mr. Norman Angell's book entitled "The (treat Illusion." In the : first xection the author challenges the almost univer- aally accepted idea that military and political power given a nation a commercial advantage. Ile claims to show that it is on economic impmaibility for one !nation to seire or destroy what creates the wealth of another, or for one nation to enrich itself by sub- jugating another. iii the second part of his book, which appears under the sub-title of the " flunian Nature of the Case," there le a further challenge, or rather a aeriee of challenges, to generally accepted ideas. These ideas PIO challenged ;nay be gathered from the writings of eminent men.

And further that society is classifying itself by interest rather than by State divisions ; that the modern Slate. losing its homogeneity ; and that all these multiple factors are making rapidly for the disappearance of State rivalries and conflicts.

Mr. Angell has undertaken a Isrge order and it would be impossible within our limits to do justice to the argument under all these claims. ; IS W•lt THE 91tODU;CT OF A LAW OF LIFE? ' There are many who, while their defence of armamenti, is based formally ow the necessity ;of preserving peace, very often the real motive, sub-conscious it may be. which prompta resistance to efforts at refwm, and which sets up a real detestation of such ; effort is the honest belief that WP should be poorer in character, and that life would be meaner, for the elimination of war and armaments. Man- kind as a whole represents the organism, and the planet the environment, to winch he is more awl more adapting himself, If struggle between mau and :min hi the true exprescrion of a universal. inexorable, unchangeable law, then the fasts and tendencies revealed by the progress of the race are inexplicable, for the drift, winch us ever becoming stronger, is away from conflict, from the use of phynical forces against our fellows, towards association and co-operation. If . we are getting at naught the natural law in thin matter . then should not the race be on its way to extinction ? ; But the very reverse is the fact, the more complete the association hatween man and man, state and attic, the greater the vitality and progress ; the more imperfect the aasociation the less the vitality and progress. SOCIETY IS CLASSIFYING_ ITSELF BY

INTERESTS RATHER THAN BY SIATE DIVISIONS. International hostilities repose for the most part upon our conception of the foreign State with which we are quarrelling as a homogeneous personality having the same character of responsibility as an individual, whereas the variety of community interest.. both material and moral, regardless of State boundaries. rend..s the analogy between nations and individuals an utterly false one. If the Louisiana cotton crop fails, a part of Lancashire starves. There is closer community of interest in isital matter between Lant.hire and Louisiana than between Lancashire and, say, the Orkneys, part of the IMMO State. . . . A Professor at Oxford will have closer community of feeling with a member of the French Academy than wish, say, a Whitechapel publican. In a thousand respects aaeociation cuts across State boundaries, which are purely conventional, and reader the biological diviaion of mankind into independent

and warring States a scientific ineptitude. Ailed factors introduced by the character of modern intercourse, have already gone far to render territorial conquest futile for the satisfaction of natural human pride and vanity. The nobleman of any country will marry the noblewoman of another more readily than a woman from a lower class of his own country. Just as in material thinga community of interest and relationthip cut clear arrant State boundaries, so inevitably will the psychic community of interest come so to do. State divisions are coining more and more to approximate to mere administra- tive divisions, leaving free scope within them, or across them, for the development of stronger bonds of interest."

It will be acknowledged that the mil existing divisions are not now 40 much between nations sa between different conception, of life and their occupa- tions. Real conflicts of the future will have little teference to State interest, but conflict's will be between democracy and autocracy, Socialism and individualism reaction and progress.or however one's nocmol meal sympathies may claseify theme divisions. It is becoming of more iniportanca to the German individualist to protect his freedom and property .Rainst the Socialists, who are attacking them. than . against the British •rtny which cannot. In the same may the Britinio Tory will lie 131,01.0 concerned with what Mr. Lloyd George's Budgets can do than Ninth . what the German can, or cannot, 010. The trades : unionist mm England in far more in aympathy with the trades unioniat in Germany than he is with the capitalist in his own country.

DOES HUMAN SATIvRE CHANGE!

We are accustomed to sock catch-phrase. as "Yon cannot change human nature." These aregenetally uttered as self-evident propositions which need no discussion. But is it not true that very profound changes, in which human nature in involved, have taken place in the habits of mankind? For ages man's life and property depended upon his club. or a well aimed atone, then ution a flint hatchet. then upon nword. then upon individual fight hedged round with the form of law, and finally upon none of these thing., but upon law alone. Thee° manifeetations of human nature have changed almost out of recognition. , These empty phrases •Lseit human nature Sr. repeated with an air of wisdom anti profundity in ! pat r.,t-like fashron IT writers of repute,and they never take the trouble to tautly the modificationn in human nature which have occurred during the memory of the present generation. We do not need to go far back in history to find a condition in which it was believed. that it was the ruler's right and duty to dictate what his fithjecte ehould !relieve, and that tbe difference,. say between Catholics and Protestant., . could only lie nettled by force. It would he as itvelligetit to nay that this could only he accomplished by force because of the unchanging character of human nature, an to hold that force is the only way to aetoln differences between nations for the same unison. • Robert Blatchford speaks of the F:riglinh people as Irving conceited, self-indulgent, decadent, and greedy, who will shout for the Empire, but will not fight for it.

The same complaint is made by the governing chimera in Germany, they bewail the spread of an attitude* of anti-militant., the steady drift away from war , amongst the people. and the extraordinary increase in the Socialist vote, which they connect with the same 'tut may Lot the sprmtil of mach a spirit in all countries he attributable to something more worthy than " self- indulgence " and " pereonal greed" ? Does it not rather spring from a conception. vague though it may be, that a settlement id s dispute by military means is one of the most foolish and wasteful means that could he adopted. Holiday Fatlllty

An inquest was held by COT 000; Brigham at the Police Station yeeterday, touching the death of Jobe Platt,the 18 years old son of James Platt,36.Plumpton- street, who was drowned in the river whilst bathing on Monday list—James Platt said be last saw his son alive on Monday afternoon. He was then in good health. The same evening he board that he had been I drowned in the river whilst bathing.—Captain Byfield, of the Runcorn Grappling Corps, Misted that he com- menced grappling for the body on Tuesday and recovered it at a quarter to seven on Thursday levening. The body was found about 300 yard' from the place of the accident.—Garrett Coleman, aged 16, Plumpton-street. mid he went with the deceased and another lad to the river, to bathe. Deceased immediately set cut for the oppoeite aide, but when about 30 yards away he sank. NCiiness jumped out of the water and ran for asaistance. Dammed was a good swimmer.— Thomas Ashurst, 54, Plimpton-street, said whilst walking along theriver bank on Monday the previous witnees ran to him and mid there wane lad in the water. He got a boat and commenced grappling but without result.—Tbe Coroner remarked that it wane enuree of satisfactpu on an occasion like the present one, to have the body recovered so into give it a Christian interment. They all recognised the work of this splendid corps which was entirely self-support- ing and had to rely upon public subecription.—The jury returned • verdict of "Accidental Death."

The Speech made the BLACK AND other day by ex-Presi- WHITE IN dent STEYN at Bloem- SOUTH AFRICA. fontein conveyed a grave warning to the white people of South Africa. It is Mr. 'STEYN'S opinion that for many years past the blacks of South Africa have been gradually losing their respect for the whites. No man within the bounds of the new Union is better able to form an opinion on the subject. Ex-President SrEYN's experience of the black man extends over a long period. It goes back to the days of LIVINGSTONE'S missionary wanderings, and bi-:I:incl. The distinguished Boer statesman is, it is evident, less afraid of the black men left to themselves than of the effects of harsh or unjust treatment of them by the whites. The newspapers, he says, now demand that every black shall suffer for the faults of a few. The reference is, no doubt, to the outcries raised of late in consequence of occasional offences against white women. Where the actual criminals are run down, and punished according to law, only good results can follow. The effect of every reasonable and just punishment in such a case must inevitably be deterrent. What is to be feared is the development of such a spirit of anger and desire for revenge as produce the lynchings in the Southern States of America. It is easy to imagine that under the conditions which exist an outrage by a solitary black man may produces vast indignation against the race. It actually does do this, in fact ; .and what ex-Presideet STEYN fears, and many more of the best minds in South Africa, is the outbreak of hasty and indiscriminate reprisals against solitary crimes such as may produce a wide, and possibly terrible revolt. It is not easy for any of us who" live at home at ease" to realise what is the actual distribution of population in South Africa, and how suddenly some event of an even worse character than the Indian Mutiny might present itself. Large as is the white population, it is vastly outnumbered among the black,great numbers of whom are friendly and may remain so if they are not attacked along with others in consequence of some fit of nerves. But there is always a possibility that the most friendly and civilised natives may "go Fantee "in a crisis. The Boers always lived in full consciousness of this possibility, and generally in a high state of preparation against it. They did, indeed, make them- selves respected, not always, it is to be feared, in commendable ways. Long familiarity with their black neighbours produced a temper of mind which kept them perpetually on the defensive. With the great increase of the white population of South Africa the whole problem has changed. There are compara- tively few cases in which the individual could defend his own if he were suddenly called upon todo so. He must—just as he would do at home—place his reliance in the law, and the force which is behind it. And the law must be administered with strict justice. The native must in some way be made to understand, as ex-President STEYN said, that the South African Union is not an enemy, but a friend and protector. Where the blacks, so enormously outnumber the whites only an impeccable administration of law can be a permanent safeguard. There must always be the greatest possible danger in outbreaks of anger, avarice or revenge.

"Payment of Members" PAYMENT OF seems to tickle the MEMBERS risibilities or arouse the ALL ROUND. covetousness of the

majority of people. It is very much open to doubt whether a poll -of the nation would be in favour of the pro- posed .£4OO per year. for M.P.'s. Meantime the smaller fry among unpaid public servants are showing keen interest in the question of payment for public service out of the public purse. Warrington Board of Guardians considered a suggestion from Toxteth Park Union that Guardians should be paid a salary of not less than £l5O per annum. Most of the members were dis- inclined to take the matter seriously—and -therein showed their good sense—the motion being defeated by 14 votes to 3. We are rather curious to know why the Rev. A. M. Mitchell favours the payment of Guardians. He proposed the resolution but advanced no argument in its favour. At Wigan, yester- day, a similar resolution was carried by the local Board of Guardians by 51 votes to eight. The prevailing idea there was that: if M.P.'s are paid, why not we? Perhaps even the humble parish councillor will have a salaried job eventually, while Town Councillors may bask in affluence in the year 2010.

Our foreign trade is still FLOURISHING booming. Comparison EXPORTS, of month by month is sometimes misleading, especially in March and April, when the variations of Easter may produce corresponding variations in the figures. May in one year is, however, fairly comparable with May in another, and the comparison of 1911 with 1910 is favourable but a longer range of comparison—that of the five months —is more instructive. During this period there has been an increase of £3,126,859 in imports, and of £17,769,078 in British exports. If it is indeed " mainly to our exports "that we must look for the test of our trade pros- perity, it is difficult to find words to express a" gain" of nearly £50,000,000 on the five months' trade as compared with the similar two years ago. During May alone, Increase in the exports of British

merchandise (that is, mainly of goods manu- factured in this country) has been £4,007,517. ICotton exports lead the way with an increase of nearly £2,000,000 on those of May, 1910; then follow most of the " ruined ' industries in a row—wool (increase on the month £312,000), chemicals (£2l7,ooo),cutlery (£lo3,ooo),and iron and steel (173,000). This last is in some ways the most interesting to those who remember the prophecy of Mr. Brailsford, chairman of" ruined" Ebbw Vale in 1903 that in a few months more the British steel trade would be " crushed out of exist- ence" by foreign competition.

All who are seriously MOTHERS AND concerned about the BABIES TO THE preservation of child- FORE. life in Warrington will

watch with -interest the progress of the Mothers' and Babies' Welcome which threw open its doors on Wednesday. The new institution is fraught With great possibilities to the public health but the first essential is that the working- class mothers, for whose benefit it is primarily intended, should take full advan- tage of it. They need not be deterred by fear of anything of an inquisitorial or unduly interfering character. The ladies who have undertaken to manage the Welcome will not attempt to dictate to mothers but will offer kindly advice in cases where they realize that they can be of practical help. If the Welcome is largely used, it will considerably facilitate the system of house visitation, which is destined to play an increasingly important part in the campaign against infantile mortality. We are glad to see the Guild of Help co operating with the health authorities in this enterprise, for there could be no more suitable sphere for the efforts of an enthu- siastic band of voluntary workers. One of the chief features of the Welcome is the periodical weighing of infants during the critical first nine months of their existence. Although it would be a mistake to emphasise mere weight unduly, it certainly is a fairly reliable index of infantile health. Homely talks will also be given on the care and management of children by the lady health visitors of the Borough Health Department.

Very considerable pro- STATE INSURANCE. gross has been made,

outside of Parliament, in bringing to a focus the difficulties which the medical profession and the Friendly Societies find in the State Insurance Bill, and thus, on the one hand, removing misconcep- tions which had given rise to imaginary difficulties, on the other hand meeting those which are substantial. It is of good augury for the complete success of the Bill, when it has reached the final state of adjustment, in satisfying all the parties concerned, that the Old Age Pensions Act entirely overcame those obstacles which Mr Chamberlain, with less patience and confidence than the Liberal Chancellor of the Exchequer, had declared to be insuperable.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

Preparations for the construction of the new Warrington Bridge are well advanced, but we understand that work on the site will not commence until Monday, the 19th inst. a..

The police, who will have a busy time during Coronation week, are not to be forgotten during the general rejoicing. They are each to receive a Coronation medal.

Mr. Harold Smith's ;word of votes cast in the 246 Parliamentary divisions since February 6th is the poorest among those of local M.P.'s. His total is 119 against 187 by Lord Wolmer,lsB by Colonel Hall Walker,and 149 by Mr. Rigby Swift.

Seven and sixpence will provide a week's holiday by the sea for a poor child. We hope Warringtonians will respond liberally to the appeal on behalf of the Children's Summer Camp, which appears in our advertising columns. No local institution is more deserving of generous support.

One of the novelties at the opening of the Mothers and Babies' Welcome on Wednes- day, was a cradle constructed out of a banana crate. It was the work of Miss Pakes, of the Health Department, and the total cost, including the cloth hangings, was 28.

In preaching at the M.anchester Cathedral, on Sunday night, Bishop Welldon expressed his thankfulness that Christians all over the world, and in Manchester, were drawing nearer to one another ; and he looked forward to the day of the Coronation, when the Non- conformists would join with them in worship at the Cathedral. •• . .

7 • Mothers and babies are receiving the most solictious attention of both the Warrington Corporation and the Guild of Help. It was particularly appropriate that at the opening of the '• Welcome 'on Wednesday the secre- tary of the Guild (Mr. Rowland) was able to present the finest baby. He evidentiy realises that example is better than precept! The Local Government Board has sanctioned the loans for the construction of 1 new bowling greens in Victoria Park and Liverpool-road, and the work is to be pro- ceeded with "immediately." St. Elphin's ?ark may also look forward to similar facili- ties in the very near future. The sanction is overdue, and the scheme ready! o _

It is no secret that the Corporation believes good horseflesh is the cheapest in the long run. We note that the Sanitary Works Committee has just decided to purchase two horses for the sum of £l.s3—which we should imagine is getting very near the top price. If our carters could not carry off prizes at the annual parade with such material to work upon, they would not be worth their salt.

The', weather has, naturally, an immediate effect on the returns from the public baths and it is rather interesting to consider the 'income for the last three fortnightly periods, I ending May 31st :—First fortnight, £l6 15s. 5d.; second, £53 is. Bd.; third, £145 9s. 4d. There is a great dearth of bathing facilities in Warrington, particularly for ladies, and the handicap must have been I severely felt during the phenomenally hot I weeks of May. • a

* * The extent to which the French bet on horse-races can be measured by the Pari- Mutuel statistics for 1910, which have just been published. The total stokes of the year amounted to .£14,980,599, or about 7s. 6d. per head of the population. Of these receipts 2 per cent. are devoted to charity, and anothor 2 per cent, to other public objects. The gain of charity from this source amounted to £299,611—a third of it (or about £100,000) going to the gratuitous medical lassistance of the poor. The cost of collection, distribution and inspection is given at rather less than £l,OOO.

Daylight saving measures are slowly but surely making headway in works and offices. Several local manufacturers alter the hours of arrival and departure of their workpeople during the summer months, and the Borough Treaeu-er's department has followed suit. Until the 30th September, the clerks will commence work at 8.30 a.m. and finish at 5 p.m. This will afford more time for recrea- tion during the summer evenings, and tend, we firmly believe, to better health and greater efficiency. A very good example of "shutting the stable door after the horse is stolen" is found in the resolution of the Street Improvement Committee requesting the owners of premises' in Kimberley-street, abutting on Sankey ' ' Brook, to fence off the land to prevent further- accidents. We fully approve of the resolu- tion and would suggest that as five small I children fell or waded into the river at Howley a week ago, and one was drowned, the Corporation might very well take steps to fence the river bank where it is used as a play-ground for children. • •

It is stated that th-e Postmaster-General has decided not to proceed with his proposal to establish a uniform design for stamps to be used throughout the Empire. The repre- sentatives of Australia and New Zealand in London just now have intimated to him that the proposal would not be received favour- ably in either Dominion, where opinion is strongly in favour of maintaining in this respect the individual characteristics of the several States. Mr. Samuel has also been told that the idea has met with an unfavour- able reception in Canada. This is only natural. _ _

Our Fire Engine Committee has been look- ing forward to having the annual demonstra- tion of the Lancashire Fire Brigades' Friendly Society in Warrington during 1912, but has had to reluctantly abandon the project owing to lack of funds. About £2OO was required for expenses, but in response to 300 circulars , issued to manufacturers and traders in the town only £2l 16s. 6d. was promised. The reason is not far to seek. Charity has made such huge demands on the private purse of the public in recent years that there cannot be much surplus for other voluntary gifts however well recommended. We sympathise with the Committee in their disappointment, but more favourable times will doubtless come!

Lord Ebury has wntten a remarkable letter which appears in the "Watford Observer." He declines to contribute to the local Coronation festivities, and the reason he gives is that since political prtfligacy is taking advantage of public torpor, to expose the civil and religions liberties of the conntry to peril, the gravity of which has had no parell.l f r centuries, I feel unable to identifY myself with rejoicings of any kind. If Lord Ebury means by "political torpor" the indifference which the country exhibits to the outcry of peers who are having to pay a very reasonable penalty for an unprece- dented attack upon national liberty we imagine that the "torpor" will not readily be dissipated.

The Rural District oouncil of Runcorn has 'agreed to co-operate with the Warrington Corporation in submitting a town planning scheme to the Local Government Board. The scheme will embrace the following parishes in the Runcorn authority's area :—Mo9re, Acton Grange, Walton Inferior and Walton Superior, Stockton Heath, Latchford- With- out, Grappenhall, Thelwall, and portions of Daresbury, Hatton, Stretton and Appleton. The Warrington Rural Council on the north, west, and east sides of the town has not yet decided to cast in its lot jointly with the borough, though the advantages to the dis- trict as a whole are obvious.

Mr. W. H. Sherburn invites us with some reason to "clear up the apparent incon- sistency" by which one man is fined £lO and costs for street betting and another becomes somewhat of a public hero by winning £19,000 in a sweepstake. It is easier to ask conun- drums than to answer them. While the law makes distinctions which may seem incon- sistent between various classes of gambling there is a marked difference between the two cases quoted by our correspondent in regard to the inherent possibilities of public harm. The street betting man is tempting poor folks to ruin, while the speculator stakes what he can afford on a chance, and does nothing worse than set a bad example. The moral and legal aspects of gambling are not likely to coincide before the millennium arrives. However regrettable the fact may be, man is a spectulative being, and when in hours of ease he will lay wagers on the respective performance of two flies crawling ups public- house window, it is evident that, despite all moral teaching, he has not learnt to include gambling in his catalogue of vices.

FOR A THAT.

Is there a luckless wight on earth, Oppressed wi' care and a' that, Who holds his life as little worth, His home is Heaven for a' that— For a' that, and a' that. There's muckle joy for a' that; He's seen the warst o' hell below, His home is Heaven for a' that.

The weary slave that drags his chain, In toil and grief, and a' that, Shall find relief from a' this pain, And rest in Heaven from a' that— From a' that, and a' that. There's freedom there from a' that, For Justice throws into the scale A recompense for a' that.

===== 0002601_19110610_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
Pair souls, in right, not unro strong,

Through love and want and a' that, There sure is power to right their wrong, And save their souls, for a' that— For a' that, and a' that. The Lord is guid for a' that ; The dell himsel' can turn and mend, And come to Heaven for a' that.

On Scotia's hills the gowans aping, The heather blooms, and a' that ; The mavis and the merle sing, But Heaven's my home for a' that For a' that, and a' that. I wadna' change for a' that. He who finds the Heaven aboon Will not come back for a' that.

Wesleyan Methodist Convention. A Wesleyan Methodist circuit convention was held at Higher Whitley on Thursday. About 200 members from all churches of the circuit journeyed either by 'bus or cycle and Quite a go d time was experienced. Mr. J. H. Clarke's farm was a capital rendezvous for such an event. In the afternoon the Rev. T. A. Turney (of Bootle) preached from the text "Be Oct ! weary in well-doing," and spoke about Christian work and its possible discouragements and difficulties. He illustrated an interesting sermon with facts from his , own experience. After tea a meeting was held ptesided over by the Rev. E. B. Keeling, who remarked that Methodists should be proud of the things which distinguished them horn other branches ,E the Christian Church. They stood for a present, free and full salvation and a high ideal of helpfulness and mutual service to mankind. The following delivered five minutes' speeches :—Rev. T. Wadding, Rev. W. Seed, Dr. Fergusson, Messrs. F. A. Harris, 3. U. , Wheatley, Frith, Muskat, thick worth, Wilcoc .n, and Twist. Several speakers referred to the valuable work which the Wesley Guild is doing in the circuit.

Stephen Noss. - • • •

Many of our reade.s will learn with regret of the death of Mr. Stephen Moss, son of the late Mr. Stephen Moss, which took place at his residence, 27, Church- street, on Sunday night after a long illness. The deceased was a pArtner in the well-known firm of S. Moss and Bone, Ltd., blacksmiths, he., Academy-street, at which establishment the deceased was a prominent figure for a large number of years. Deceased who was 32 years of age, leaves a widow and two children to mourn his loss. The funeral took place on Wednesday at the Warrington Cenet..ry, a large number attending to pay their last tribute of respect. The servive at the graveside was conducted by the Rev. J.ll. G. Hollingworth. Trams, Croquet, Cricket ant other ootdoor lames.— J. S. GARLAND, 51 to 55, Bridge en, Warrington --Advt. Notable Success of Crosfleld's Baud. Messrs. Crosfield's famous Perfection Soap Works Band achieved a notable victory on Saturday at the Darwen Brass Band Contest, when they succeeded in winning outright the Bentham Challenge Shield and Cup, having now won it three times in succession. There were eight entries, and the test piece was " Crispino." In addition to securirg the shield the band was awarded three gdd medals for the best solo cornet, soprano cornet and euphonium and a special pine for the best trombone. Since 1906 the band has won the following prizes at this contest :-1906. 3rd : 1907, 2nd ; 1905, Ist; 1910, let; 1911, lat, The band, in which Mr. A. H. Crosfield has taken a very keen interest ever since its formation eleven lyears ago, has obtained a position of eminence in the competitive world and another successful season is , anticipated. In 1910 their record was unblemished by a single failure, a prize being brought home at every venture. The record was seven firsts, one second, two thirds and two fourths. For 1911 their achievements up-to-date are three tirstaand one second. The band will compete in the New Brighton Tower band contest to-day (Saturday.) Mr. William Halliwell, of Wigan, is the conductor of the band, Mr. E. Smith the bandmaster and Mr. Joseph Taylor the secretary.

SALARY INCREASE.

It is proposed that the salary of Mr. H. B. Jackson chief assistant electrical engineer, be increased from t,.£180 per annum from the lat Juno.

Bank Holiday. Favoured with perfect summer weather, the Whitsuntide Bank Holiday of 1911 has been a record one from the holiday-making point of view. Most people who could conveniently escape from the monotony of town life responded to the call of the sea or of the country-side, which is just now looking at its best. In the early hours of Monday the railway stations were besieged with crowds anxious to avail themselves of the numerous excursions which the companies had arranged, and during the greeter part of the day the town looked deserted. At Bank Quay station. the booking. were the heaviest experienced on Whit-Monday for many years past, well-filled trains departing at frequent intervals for Blackpool, Morecambe, the Lake District and North Wales. A similar report comes from Central Station when the principal bookings were : —New Brighton. 7011; Southport, 601.1; Liverpool, 1,600; Manchester, GOi. In Warrington ilea& there were no extra holiday attractions but in the evening the various placer of amusement were well patromed.

Profound Religious Significance of the

'Coronation

Amidst the natural excitement connected with this event, and the general looking forward to a day of sport and pleesore, we trust, (says St. Mary's Gres*, Sankey Magaxine) that our parishioners will boar in mind that our Kin/ and Queen on that day are going to God's /louse to be crowned, and to pray that God will guide and help .d strengthen them to fulfil worthily their high destiny. We also, their subjects, abould meet to prey likewise. This is the point to keep in view. The Coronation is an act of pmfound religious significance, and if it he accompanied by tbe prayers of the faithful ia all parts of the Empire, not only may we expect that God's special blessing will rest upon our King, but that he will receive the I peculiar gifts tint he needs for ruling his vast dominione at this critical moment of tboir history.

The Antiquity of the Coronation Service• Holy Trinity Parish Madatine ha. the following : The greet event that will overshadow every other thia month is the Coronation of cur gracious Sovereign King George in Westminster Abbey. It has for more centuries been • great and solemn function in Christian countries, and can be traced back to A.D. 457, when I.eo WY crowned hy Amatolion the patriarr h Constanti- nople. The first Coronation to England was that of gig hirthi King of Hernia ; end we have still the forma used in the time of the Ileptarchy, from which our Coronation service is sutetentiully derived. " It is a form of immemorial prescription," says Palmer, "fled nanctioned by the solemn approval of all the states of the realm, the nobility, the clergy and the people 4s:entitled at its celebration. The prayers are framed in the best spirit of antiquity, with the rhythm no characteristic of primitke forms, and with an elevation and majesty ot sentiment unsutpessed in any part of our litorgy. The service is, however, peculiarly valuable, as recording certain high religious and political principles, which thus receive the full sanction of the Church and nation; sad especially there is an acknowledgment of the Sovereignty of Christ over the whole world, and the derivation of all kindly power from Him in the following words torldre”.tl to ! him at the giving of the Orb and the Cross— • When ! you see this Orb thus set under the Ones remember ' that the whole world is subject to the power and Empire of Christ our Redeemer.' " (Palmer in Hook's Dictionary).

There Is much at the present time that make. this service obit of special iroportunce and significance, and sae have no doubt that the 111•01/10 throughout the country will ensemble in their churches to make the celebration a memorable one.

Sporting turn--and indeed the public generally— will find much to interest them in 'Flyaway's Derby a farcical flutter in five seta," which forms the prig, cipal item on the bill at the hippodrome this week. ! The show is quite a pretentious one, employing a company of about thirty people and four fine race- horses. Quite a story is woven round the lierby " and the owners of two rival racehorses, and there is plenty of fun, frolic, and even music, before Flyaway actually wins the classic spi,ting event in true flyaway fashion. Mr. Jimmy Learmouth greatly dis- tinguishes himself as a comic policemen and keeps the house in roars if laughter while he is

on." The final scene presents a thrilling climax, with the four thoroughbreds tearing along at full gallop over a inoviog platform, amid a thunder of hoofs and a hurricane of cheering which are most !effective and realistic. A distinctly novel turn is that

of Austin Latham, the novelty cartoonist, who works with Smear paint" on the glans fl a lantern which reflects the pictures on a screen at the back of the stage ' and behind the artist. The quickness of Mr. Latham's work, and the accuracy and fulness of hit portraiture I are remarkable, lie earned some very hearty applause. Larola presents a clever comedy juggling act, and Eric balvere and l'hil Wallace work considerable ingenuity into a turn comprising good claim vocalism mind patter. Gordon Stretton fulfils hit description of "artistic coon," and to a couple of attractive sings adds dancing of a delightfully light-footed character. Chan Erard pays a return visit and quite suits the audience with his mimiery and original pia:lndio accomplishments. Harry ilaymond and his >f ung slaughter Ivy, excel in eccentric combined dancing, but the father's vocaligm is hardly equal to his ootwork. Mr. ‘liddop's orchestra performs excellently both in accompiniments And band wdectiona.

NEW BISHOP OF SOUTHWARK.

With impressive ceremonial, the Rev. Dr. Hubert Barge (late of Winchester College), bas been installed as Bishop of Southwark, at Southwark Cathedral. The Mayor, Aldermen, and Councillors Leaded the procession, then came the Choir, the Clergy of the Diocese, Cancels, and Bishops. One of the quaint incidents in the enthronement Ia the fact that the Bishop is the last person to be admitted to the Cathedral. The door is closed against him, and he has to knock upon the great outer doors three times with his staff in order to be admitted. At the conclusion of the ritual, Dr. Sorge delivered • very forceful address on the work of the church. Our picture shows the peocvsion entering the Cathed al, soda detachment of Boy Scouts as guard of honmir.

=I,,PRII,LAGES

THROUGH THE PEAK DISTRICT.

The Land of Robin Hood and Little John.

We are indebted to Mr. John Naylor, a local " pilgrim " of much renown, for the " pilgrimage " described in the following article. We hope that other descriptive sketches may follow. Opinions differ as to what areas comprise the Peak District, and, ignoring the views of the Ordnance surveyors and local authorities, Mr. Baddeley, author of one of the best guides to that region, makes the term cover " the . . . .

whole of the, hill-country of North Derby- shire, and such parts of the adjacent coun- ties as physically belong to it.' Peakland .cenery is of such a different type of beauty from that of the highlands of our own county, and it possesses such peculiar charms that I would urge familiarity with it to all local cyclists who are keen lovers of the beautiful

in landscapes. Do-v-isia-ii,-p—eria;;, holds the pride of primary place in Derbyshire scenery, but the little round I am following hereafter will reveal more varied aspects of the beauties of mountain, moorland, and valley to be found in a county of such diversity of scenery. The local cyclist may find it convenient to take the train via Stockport to this place, which is just on the border of Cheshire and Derbyshire. From Whaley Bridge one highway leads, by a long climb over the hills, to Buxton, a direct route. Another road also makes for Buxton, by way of Chapel-en-le-Frith, and it is this road we shall follow. It is an exceedingly pleasant and easy road to th 6 'capital of the Peak," as Chapel-en-le-Frith is entitled to be called. On the right rise the uplands adjacent to Coombs Moss, on the left the commanding ridge of Eccles Pike. The valley between is pleasant and verdurous, and holds, in its hollow, an ostensive mere, a reservoir for canals, called Coombs Reservoir. One may get a glimpse of the beauty of this lake near a trim old wayside inn, approaching Chapel-en-le-Frith, with the sign of the "Hanging Gate," bear- the usual intimation and the invitation to wayfarers to "refresh themselves and travel on."

_ . Before reaching the inn a farmhouse on the hillside to the right of the highway is " Tunstead," a haunted house, containing a grim relic in the shape of "Dicky's skull," the history of which may be gathered by the curious from any of the local residents. Chapel-en-le-Frith, for all its great antiquity "is dull and featureless," and beyond the town the highway rises towards Doveholes, through a little belt of woodland, and at a road junction we leave the Buxton route and take the highway to the left for a climb to Rushup Edge. The summit gained, a little plateau of uniform surface provides a wel- come spin of two miles on the level with lovely moorlands on each side.

===== 0002601_19110610_art0080.txt | included=true | included =====
CASTLETON.

Then (30111118 the descent into Castleton, by way of a road which, I believe, costs more per mile for upkeep than any other high- way in Derbyshire. The entrance to some of the "mines" for which Castleton is famous may be seen on the descent. Half-way down the road takes a sudden and dangerous turn round that mysterious hill Mam Tor, or shivering mountain," which, owing to the shaly nature of its composition, is frequently liable to landslides.

A beautiful view of the valley in which Castleton lies unfolds itself on the descent, with Win Hill and Lose Hill to the left of it, and the ruined castle, Peveril of the Peak, a little above the town to the right. Castleton has so many features of interest, it calls for a considerable break in the excursion. I can- not do more than briefly mention its attractions here.

On the descent into the town the entrance to the Blue John Mine is passed on the right. Here the stone called "Blue John '• is obtained, and here abound fine specimens of stalactites and stalagmites. Then we come to the Speedwell Mine, where one can be rowed in a boat underground to what is called the Bottomless Pit, a most romantic I experience, and finally the Great Cave of Castleton, with its rope-walk entrance—one of the finest caves in the British Isles. Above this stands the romantic castle of Peveril, so that the cyclist should allow a few hours to visit these sights, which on no account should be missed. Indeed, one could well spend a day in Castleton, and not find it too long for sight-seeing.

===== 0002601_19110610_art0081.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GRAVE OF LITTLE JOHN.

Beyond Castleton the white highway leads to the little village of Hope, whose church, with a somewhat squat tower and thick spire may be observed ahead. Hence the road, always easy, runs in the valley alongside the little river Noe, which joins the Derwent at Mytham Bridge Inn, and from this place still follows the river (now the Derwent), until near Hathersage,when it turns abruptly to the left. The route gives one the oppor- tunity of enjoying some of the quieter pastoral scenery of Derbyshire, which is in strong contrast to the wild landscape between Chapel-en-le-Frith and Castleton. " I was bound for Hathersage," writes a correspondent of mine "for I had read a history of Robin Hood,. years ago, in which Little John's real dame was given as my own, and I had looked on him ever since as probably one of my most worthy ancestors. Hathersage, since it has been made more accessible from the great manufacturing towns which lie within a radius of forty miles or so from it by a branch of the Midland Railway, has taken profit of its great natural beauty of situation, and it is now a place much visited by the good folk of Sheffield, ',Manchester and districts. The church is finely placed on rising ground north of the village, and I had a stiff climb to reach it. I found the grave, which had been modernised in recent years, but the old head and foot stones are still there, with the old yew trees planted years ago above them. The distance between these stones, signifies that Little John, if this be indeed his grave, was a very _tall man. -

Alongside the churchyard, and at a lower elevation, stands the Bell Inn, through the bedroom window of which, the story goes, Little John shot an arrow to mark the place where he wished to be buried. It is only a very short distance from the window to the, grave, so that the old freebooter's arms must' have been very weak if it was intended as is-1 stray shot. Robin Rood seems to be associated with parts of the country, and on the Church Stretton Hills I notice there is a place called Robin Hood's Butts' while there is a 'Robin Hood's Well' on the Great North- road in the neighbourhood of Doncaster.

FAMOUS HCKNERY. From Hathersage the cyclist may drop down the valley of the Derwent to Grindle- ford, but for my part I would counsel a climb to Fox House Inn, a famous hostelry on the high moorlands, which are upheld by Stanage Edge and White Edge. Some of the most

remarkable scenery in Derbyshire is to be found on this little journey, and the prospect from points of vantage on the climb will not soon be forgotten. This is a very ancient highway from Sheffield to Castleton, and some of the gradients on the threemiles to Fox House are pretty stiff, but the road is excel- lently engineered. Half-way up, where the road makes a sudden turn to the left between two rocks appropriately called the" Surprise," a backward glance reveals "the handsomest view in the Midland Counties. The main features are Hope Dale, wide and pastoral, in front,and the valley of the Derwent,narrow and beautifully wooded, reaching far away to the south. Between the two is a sylvan V-shaped glen, down which the Highlow Brook flows to its confluence with the Derwent. In the foreground green meadows slope steeply to the riverside,and here again as we look down upon a beautiful reach of tife river spanned by a rustic bridge, our thoughts hark back to the Teith an Callendar Bridge, as seen from Ben Ledi— twin scenes of beauty, such as an artist might make his name and fortune by faith- fully depicting." It is a good few years ago since I first saw this picture, guided hither by a good Salopian whose adopted home lay on the banks of the mere below Coombs Moss; but this one revelation of that July ride is in lively memory still. The road now turns abruptly to the north- west, and climbs to Burbage Bridge where, a few yards away, a block of gritstOne, bear- ing before and behind a marvellous likeness to a toad, is called the Toad's Mouth. On the further way some exceeiingly bold rock scenery marks the outlook, the culminating ridge being called Biggar Tor. A footpath leads to a group of rocks, Carl Wark, which , bear strong evidence of human interference, , and are well worth a visit. Indeed, the , route is as full of interest as it well may be. A mile or so beyond Fox Hotel the turning to the right is taken, and subsequently the cyclist has a delightful run down Froggatt Edge to the valley of the Derwent, passing on the way that delightfully-situated inn, The I Chequers, overlooking a charming reach of ' the river. After crossing the river the high- ; way through Stony Middleton and Tideswell is struck, offering a profitable and highly interesting route back to Chapel-en-le-Frith i , and Whaley Bridge, by way of remote Peak I Forest.

A LETTER FROM A PRETTY BIRD. At this time of the year most boys spend a little time in looking for birds nests. There is not the least harm in that so long as the nests are not disturbed ; indeed it is very good that boys should get to know as much as they can about the sweet little singers and workers of the bird creation. Perhaps both the boys and girls will like to read a very interesting letter from one of the smallest and prettiest of birds, the tiny creature we call a Gold-Crested Kinglet. "Dear Young Friend.,— No doubt you like to read about Idriers, everybody doer, and.. I am a Fe%i living fairy king, whom you probably have often seen and heard and wondered about, I have made up my mind, although lam the: smallest bird in Europe, to write you a If tter. I do ' not live in the thimble of the folghvers, like some of the fairies you have read about, but whenever I can am ng the net dies of the pine. " I belong to the most ancient royal [marry in the wo Id, all my ancesto n for thousands of years having been crowned kings and queens of Birdie Land. When the first Coronation took place history does not record, but doubtless it errs as fairies reckon, once upon a time, and ever since millions and mi:lions of little innocent intelligent feathered beings, the beautiful and perfect thfughts of CA, have worn the solder] crowns placed upon our tiny heads by the King of Heaven, arid quierly and cheer fully per- formed the work for which we were created. Gold- crested kinglets my be called the citizens of the world, as we are found in Europe, Asia. Africa and America, from the cold arctic north to the nrild sunny south. Everywhere we •-e acknowledged as the kings of Birdie Land ; indeed we have nearly as many royal names as the Emperor of China. Here are a few —Fairy king, snow king, king bird, little king king of the hedge, gold crested warbler, golden- crowned wren, and gold crested kinglet. " Gold-crests are all kiegs and queens who are dressed in the daintiest and prettiest robes you can imagine. Our coats are of yellowish green.watsicoats cream-coloured, wings and tails russet-brown, edged with yellow. Across our wings aretro pale yellow j stripes, and over each eye a bow or et black, wi h a little golden crown set on our heads between them, and as we woodland fain i flit about among the branches of our favourite larch, fir, or pine trees, we set up the feathers of our tiny airy fairy crowns or lay them flat to express the feelings of our happy hearth and no that no may he quite comfortable and have no ',frijoles of dust getting into our nostrils as we nimbly run about, up and around the trunks of trees in search of insects and their eggs,* tiny feather covers each opening. This is one of the distinguish- ing marka of die family. Every year during the months of October and November we gold-crente make up little travelling parties of 30 or 40 and go ti spend the winter months in warmer localities. Those in Norway and Sweden come flying over the stormy North Sea to Britain, although how they manage if no one can understand, they are such fragile creatures, not much more than half the one of your Jenny Wren, and seeming no more than a little fluff of feathers, and yet, year after year they come in thousands, stay here till February, and then return to their northern homes to build their fiesta and rear their young. I am a native of the North of Scotland, but I have been living in England all the winter, and now I am on my way borne to attend to family matters. My queen is quite anxious to bip house-building Every year she makes a lovely little nest of fine moss, wool, cobwebs, and the small leaves of the fir and lines it with feathers, all then ice soft tips turned inwards, and when it is finished she lays day after day • tiny egg not much larger than a pea. When there are eight or ten she site patiently on them till the royal babies appear. and then we are oh! so happy, happy. We feed them and guard them, and speak softly and gently to them, and love theta with 'all our heart.. .

"Please do not let anybaly elm° to touch, or frighten an: we are God's little 1 irdies, and lie knows where we all live and makes as to be happy."

And would you know' A pleasant aong to me replied, " For someone else we sing, And this is why the woodlands wide With rapture round us sing! " 0 daisies crowding all the fields. And twinkling gram and buds that grow ! Each glance you greet With smiles no ;meet ! "And why? Ah, would you know ?" Their beauty to my heart replied, " For someone else we live, And nothing in the world an wide Is sweeter than to give!" SOME SCHOOLBOY ANSWERS.

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

AN EYE FOR AN EN. 'the author made the correction in the proof, but the too scrupul as printer could not bring himself to follow such unheatd of marking ; and the writer finally uppaled to the publisher, whereuron, of course, the "comp' was obligtd ti yield. SPIRITUALISM.

The arrangements for the appearance cf an eminent medium at the Druids Hall recalls the criticism that Spiritualists have weakened the •alue of their testimony as to the world beyond by ',mounding it with a Howl of rather undignified circumstances. The fact is that our Spiritualist friends, at all events in Warrington, have done good service by insisting up n and emphasising as important scriptural truth which has been strangely neglected by orthod a churches. There are certain things I should like to have written Oil the gel eral subject, but I must hasten along. VALUE _OF SCIENCE.

In " The Doctor's Dilemma," G.B.S. makes one of his charm:a. exclaim : " Lord, ye. ! Modern science is a wonderful thing. Look at your great discoveries. Where are they leading to? Why, right bock to my poor dear old father's ideas and discoveries. He's been dead now theme 40 years. 111 it's very interesting." An was observed in last " Notes and Notions," there is nothing are under the sun—in a sense ; I once made no diection of facts and incidenta tending in a remarkable degree to demonstrate the profundity of the truth underlying that raying of the ancient Far. At the same time, modern science inn more wonderful thing than the Shavian philosopher jus. quoted would seem to imply. Just drink ho in our indurrries what were once regarded as absolutely waste products are nowalaye utilised. Then what a revelation in that respect one of our agricultural colleges would be to an old-time farmer mild one re-visit the glimpses of the 1111,09 ! Priestley, who is lecturer in botany at Bristol University, detailed various experiments which have been carried out in Bristol and Evesham. showing how the application of electrical treatment to growing wbeat, encumber plants, marigolds, and strawberry plants had greatly increased the veld. But what need have we of further rvitness than " Redcap " himself? He has quite recently given readers of this journal abundant evidence in favour of what is here advocated. THE HALF-TIMER. _

Mr. Snowden's reierence to the projected abolition of half-time labour took me hick in thought. *core of years ago, when a oertain Chamber of Commerce engaged me to assist in the promotion oft monetre petition to Parliament protesting spinet the raising of the age limit in respect of juvenile operation. in the mills of Lancashire and Yorkshire. A I'EEP BEHIND THE :sCENE4. _

While I am about it, I may confess that about the period just referred to I happened to be out of employment for six months or 80. I shall reiptire a receipt for tiff, however." I asked "And what shout the difference? " " Oh, well, you know, that will go into my pocket." "An what? " I ventured (nab, to enquire. DROWNING FATALITY.—A sad drowning fatality occurred on Thursday evening at Feernhead. Joni. Monks, aged 21, of Station-road, Padget°, left the house at 6.30 to go for a bathe in the clay pits, Fearn. head. As one his habit he took with him soap and towel. A boy named Clarke who wes hathing in an adjoining pit noticed the deceased'. clothes in the bank and seeing nothing of Monks he immediately gave an alarm at the police station. Another man named James Ifidighton diveeted himself of hie clothes and continually dived into the pits for an hour, but bin efforte failed to find the whereabouts of the body. On the arrival of the police dragging operations were commenced and carried on through the night, the body being recoverod at eight o'clock yesterday morning by P.C.'. Key nod Itiitterworth. The depth of the water at the spot where the deceened was found was about 20 feet deep and it is supposed that he was seized with cramp.

C, Mr. J. Srevnti a behalf of the Association of House and Hhip Pointers, lie. to protest agaiest the decision of the Education Committee not to have the 'work of decorating and painting the schools done in , the Winter when the paintiag trade is sleek, and points oat that neighbouring authorities have shown more ortaideration-la the miter. -An Peace 'or War? .bir,-31t. C. E. parker's contributions to your paper , ? Pe ac e or wrai is • valuable analysis of a book of its atimuleting thoughts and Masterly phiaseology is worthy of earnest study. I have read many reviewe of Mr. Angell's book, but Mr. Parker's is the fairest and most broadminded, and twrite this letter in no spirit of antagonism, but •so a student anxious to strive at the truth. For on sty first reading of " The Great ". illusion.? I was completely carried away by its, , convincing logic and obvious sincerity. After more careful readings the following queations among many others remain unanswered. I,,would be very kind of Mr. Parker, if in his final sM•vey he could suggest , ware reply. 1. Are wars fought simply because they are supposed to pay? Has not the economic motive so dazzled Mr. Angell that be has not made sufficient allowance for such factors as the ambition of a king , or a ruling caste, the irrationality of.,azistions, the sentimeotal attachment to native land, e.g.tAtleanse, Lorraine? 2. Even supposing Germany lost financially after the Franco-Prussian, was not this poasiblvloss counterbalanced by the political stability formed by the unifying influence of the war ? . .3. Suppose this country smashed and no direct profit obtained' by our conquerors. What however , • , ! becomes of our oversee. trade? If we are defeated at •• home, factoties close, men starve, trade and credit are dislocated. Our export trade must stop. Who sells the goods tvhich we are unable to supply? :Finally may I point out that Mr. Angell agrees , with the fundamental principles of National Service • . that self defence and preparation for war are justifi- able. His words on p. 278 might well have been penned by a member of the N S.L. "I deem that Ire or any Other nation ere justified in taking means of twit-defence to prevent such aggression. This is not, , therefore, a plea for disarmament irrespective nf the • action of other nations. So long as current political ' philosophy in Europe remains what it is, 1 would not urge the reduction of our war budget by a single • sovereign."—Yours etc., B. S. TOWNROE.

What is Gambling? Sir,—" The great majority of men to-day take their opinions and belief° much more willingly from the modern press than from the average pulpit." This is one of the reasons frequently given for the decline of formal religion as represented by the various, more alms, orthodox churches as shown by the persistent &cline in membership and attendance. Doubtless there is truth in the statement—especially since social, moral and religious questions have been discussed so fully, ably, and rationally in the more 12101.10118 journals. For all that, however, young men who may look to the press for definite and consistent leading must expect to meet many a diaappointment as well lissome perplexing puzzles during the search. Almost any issue of a good average newspaper will supply ample proof of the truth of this statement. Take gambling as • subject and your issue of the 3rd inst., for example. On page 8 in the police court news you report that a man suns convicted of the crime of gambling (betting inns entry) and lined 5:10 and costs. Many will say "Serve him right." - On page 12 we find a very detailed and illustrated account of a win—or as pa term it" Windfall"—of some thousands of pounds in what seems to have been rightly named by one leading journal "The biggest gamble in lie world." Here is the thinking young man's puzzle. Will he not ask—" What makes the one a criminal action and the other n windfall ?" "Is it simply the size of the transaction ?" If not—what is gambling anyway? If it means speculating in games of chance such as pitch and toss, hanker, sweepstakes, Au, then surely the latter is more downright gambling than the former, seeing that the invesior of a shilling with a bookmaker has his own choice ot a horse for his investment (which may imply some skill) while the other " chance " is but like the toss of a coin. Perhaps, however, as you have given so fine an advertisement to the " windfall ' you may be able to clear up the apparent inconsistency. Many of your readers would like to see your explanation, amongst whom will be yours sincprely, W. H. BRERBURN. Rotherwood, Stockton Heath, Warrington. 7th June, 1911.

The Teachers and the Coronation. Sir,—The teachers meeting on Thursday evening last week dealt chiefly with the point of rue "Selfishness " in not acceding to the wishes of the Coronation Committee, in the carrying out of their. scheme, and thus the impracticability of the scheme , was somewhat ignored oldie meeting. May I therefore try to show bow in many ways the idea of a grand assembly of 11,000 children from four years old at Victoria Park on Coronation Day from two to five is absolutely impracticable and ioadvisable. 1. Phe Friendly Societies' procession in the morn- ing is, I believe, due to terminate at 1.30 and as the children and their mothers will naturally wish to see that procession it would be obviouely impossible for the children to be at Victoria Park at 2 in any case, as dinner roust be negotiated, and then a two miles walk in procession covered by someschools to get to the Park.

- ---• 2. The fact that only one narrow road is available for over 12,000 of the scholar', with Warrington Bridge to cross, was well treated at the meeting no I , simply etate it. 3. The tea proposed (bun or buns and aerated * drinks) would be m,st. inadequate for children (many • it V, • of whom w,uld hove had to start out without dinner) , • to suffice them from 1.0 to 6 0 p.m. which is the earliest time poseible for arrival at home. 4. The sanitary arrangements at the Park are totally unfit for this number of people for several hours, especially when we consider that the schools were to be allotted places enclosed, no that the scholars could not wander away. 5. The unfenced state of the river bank is certainly a great danger which needed careful consideration. 6. The tableaux proposed would naturally draw a great number of the pubic into the Park, anti infants' teachers especially, would have a very anxious time with mothers, who, according to the Coronation • • Committee's ideas, were to be kept away from their •••40 ellkr . children. A , alio 7. 'rho re-assembling of the children for the return r • Are ", • ' home (for the children were to be marched back to the various schools) would, I imagine, be an impos- sible task, however much isolat on were enforced, for , the distribution of the u.as would dislocate largely any school's arrangement, and the sorting out of 3,000 infants in 11,000 children (without counting encroaoli- ,• ing relative.) for the return journey would be impos- sible. A. The return home was to commence at 5 pm. but to te-assemble the schools, however expeditiously, and then to march them beck among the crowds returning to town, would to my mind take hours, eapecially as we have again only Knutsford-road and Warrington 13ridge, These last two items, to my naiad, are fatal to the whole scheme. But if we left the children to find their own way home what chance would small boys and girls from Sankey Bridges and Town's End have of reaching home safely and in reasonable time ? O. Last, but by no means least, comes the fact that Walking Day falls next day; the children must be at the schools at Sam,, and anybody with an eye on past Walking Days would say that Walking Dv sufficiently tiring by itself, without the walk to • Victoria Park, and the " standing around" there, for three hours being enforcid on the preceding day. Then the clothes need mention for moth,e cannot afford two sets of " Walking Day "clothes, and the first day would spoil the secnd for many parents, who takes pride in their children's turn-out, on that (to them) day of days. Hoping that our actions in criticising, and so apparently scotching this impractic- able scheme may meet with the approval of the War- rington parents—l am, yours truly, C. TWINER.

Physiognomy and Phrenology. SW:- My friend "An Old Journalist," like many others, is glad to shelter behind suggestions of " guesswork" as a defence when confronted with psychic hwts he is unwilling to accept. What clever guessers thews "shrewd old fortune-tellers" Ac., must be! I haven't time this week to write as fully as I would like, but I would ask : If the brain is the organ for the exercise of thought and action, wby should not the outward signs of its development indicate character as well as the face? Students knov that thl, is so, but those who have not studied the subject, instead of adopting the agnostic position, and admitting that they don't know, jump at conclu- sions, which they proceed to put before the world as knowledge .d try to make other people think that because they say it is or is not so it Must or must not be so. Investigators ate compelled to admit that Hamblet's aphorism still holds good. There are more things in heaven and earth than are dreamt of in their philosophy.
