The Council and the Suffrage.

(Thscrver, whose style I recognise as that ota gentleman with whom! have already correaponded in the " Lancashire Daily Post," ia at pains to explain away the value of the _Warrington Town Council . . •

Resolution. He says :—" The value of these resolu- tions is simply the value attaching to the opinions of individuals, and not of the burgesses or citizens they represent." 'The Editor of the Anti-Suffiage Review (February, 1911) takes an opposite view of Town Council resolu- tions "It is significant," he says " that these resolutions route almost exclusively from bodies which depend to an appreciable extent for their election upon the votes of women. The pressure exerted upon the Council may be unconscious, but is none the less real. The excavators of Herculsnendllfound upon the wall of a house the following imperialiable election address Vote for Proclinius to-day, and he will vote for you to-morrow.' Homan nature has not changed much, and • something for something ' remitue as powerful a motive as ever." (I may add in panning that no more powerful argument in favour of sides for women has ever been put forward by Suffragists). Now which is right—" Observer," or his Editor? I em personally inclined to think that individual members of Town Councils and the people that they represent, are at one on the suffrage question. As individuals and public representatives, the way is clear for Town Councillors.

. -- The one point in my recent letter!, which Mr. Beaumont and "Observer" might justly have attacked, they have failed to attack. The publication in a certain paper, from which I quoted, about the Shottermill Anti-Suffrage canvass ha. proved to be hasty and inaccurate, and hms been publicly acknow- ledged to be an, as is only fair, by Suffragists. This fact seen,s to be unknown to Mr. Beaumont and " Observer."

I have never undertaken, as" Observer " suggests, to discredit every dotal of the Anti-Suffrage canvass. Such a task would be colossal, more unnecessary,and, I hope impossible. . .

Beyond what Anti-Suffragists toll us, I know I nothing of how the Anti-Suffrage canvass was con- I ducted in N.W. Manchester, the only place which Mr. Beaumont deigns to name ; but the fact that Sir G. Kemp, M.P., for that district, soya that he believes his constituents are with him on the Suffrage question, seems to grow that he regards the canvass as a negligible quantity. "Observer" says it is "absurd " to say that the more Mr. Beaumont talks of this, the better for us. Naturally, I fail to see the absurdity. "Observer's"inaccuracy in stating that Mr. Beaumont challenged as to debate in Warrington is indicative of his whole correspondence, here and elsewhere. It was I, on behalf of Suffragists here, who challenged Mr. Beaumont to debate, but somehow or other he does not seem keen on it Perhaps if "Observer"came and supported him,it would give him greater confidence. We should be delighted to have both exponents of Anti-Suffragism here. Yours etc. L. F. WARING.

Progressive Finance (P)

Sir—Your heading of a report in a iecent issue of a meeting held under the auspices of the Progreseive Labour Leagueacclaiming progressive finance set me asking myself as to whether we really had a , thoroughly progressive financial policy. And when I say progressive I do not compare my standard of progressive finance to something that has been but to something that ought to be Our National yearly bill now amounts to a sum of about £170,000,000, and the first question the progressive financier should ass him- self or herself is whether this sum is raised by fairly opportionirig t each individual member of the com- munity a tax which is in prop,tion e fair contribu- tion to the nation's expenses, or whether it is levied in such a manner as t., make it possible for unscrupulous persons to pass his tax on to others. Or again : whether the nation's money is spent in a way advantageous to the community? Our source of revenue is by direct and indirect taxation. We tax land, rents, incomes, f, od,stamp,,Ac., with the result that, according to Mr. Chiozza Money in 1907 (and thore is very little change in principle in spite of the "revolutionary budget,") for each £1 of income we 987 A. Very Rich. 75,000 people, each with an income of over £5,000 per annum, is. 7d. B. Well-to-do. 1,750,000 people with incomes from £7OO to £5,000 per annum, Is. 91d. C. Middle and Upper Middle Classes. 4,000,000 people with incomes from £l6O to £7OO per annum, le. 0101 D. Working and Lower Middle Classes. 38, 7501.100 people, nil to £l6O per annum, ls. 3d. As a workingman I protest against the injustice of such taxation and as a progreseive I protest againat such finance being classed as progressive. Could any taxation be deemed fair such as the rich man being taxed about 5.8 out of a weekly income of £lOO leaving him 102 a week to live on and the poor man being taxed Is. 3d, out of his weekly wage of El leaving him 18s. 9d. to evict upon ? To one,tasation is • tolerable inconvenience,to the other an intolerable burden. Again, as a progressive I think whilst we have such an unequal distribution of wealth the vrorkingclass ought not to pay any taxation whatever. "The Daily Mill Year Bock" of 1908 e•timates the nation's yearly income at 11,750,000,000, £850,000,000 taken by 5 million pereons and £900000,000 taken by 39 million persons. Whilst such an anomaly in wealth distribution exists I think such a few people enjoying such • huge income ought to pay the national bill, not only as a matter of economic justice, but because a greater portion of such money is spent for purpoaes from which they receive the greatest benefits. So much for the manner in which we gather the nation's income. A good business man would insist that any money seed in a business would be spent in a productive way, but a scrutiny of the expenditure provides us with the fact that at least 110 millions is spent in a manner that la unproductive. Interest paid on the national debt amounts to about 28 millions. The cost of the army and navy amounts to about 70 millions. Civil administration, which includes pensionn for services said to have been useful to the nation, and royalty grant, about 38 millions, mak leg only about 50 millions spent in State adminie- tration after paying local taxation dues. For practical purposes the cost of interest on national debt may be added to that of cost of Army and Navy, since, with the exception of Suez Canal shares, the total national debt has been spent for war purposes. It will be seen that we are spending about 100 millions a year fir the purposes of blowing one another to atoms. A progesaive financial programme would sin] at rade, nag this item of national expenditure, which is not the case. The critic may argue that this cost is inevitable. This is not my point. What I nay is that while we spend our money as we do now instead of developing the resources of the country in, say, buying out the railways for the nation's use and not for private individuals' profit, in town planning, in preventing coons erosion, State afforestation and other things, we have not a progressive national financial system—Years LABOR OMNIA VINCIT.

MR. HARRY BROUGH• AND MISS LILLIE BIRTLES.

Brough—Birtles

, Christ Church, Pad—gate, was the scene a a very 'pretty wedding, which aroused great interest in the district on Wednesday afternoon, when Mr. Harry Brough, son of the late Councillor Walter Brongh and of Mrs. Brough, The Grange, Fearnheed, was married to Mime Nina Elizabeth (Lillie/ !dirties, daughter of Councillor T. Birtles, Brentwood, FearnLead. The bride and bride. groom are members of two of the best-known and highly-respected families in Padgate and the esteem in which they are peraottally held found expression in the very large congregation attending the ceremony. Ideal . weather prevailed and the bright sunshine heightened the beauty of the scene in the church which had been most tastefully decorated with lilies, gladioli and white sweet peas. The officiating clergy were the Rev. W. Pollock Hill, of Aahton-in-Makerfield, and the Rev. a. W. Dickinson, vicar of Padgate. The service was fully choral, and included the hymns" 0 Perfect Love " and 'The voice that breathed o'er Eden." Mr. T. Blackwell was at the organ and played festal music including Mendelssobu's Wedding Match. The bride, who was given away by her father, wore a rich ivory white satin princess dress veiled with ninon. 'rho bodice was daintily embroidered with pearls and beads, and a loose train of satin hung from the shoulders She also wore a fine tulle veil surmounted with real orange blossom. Her bouquet was composed or white roars, lilies and white heather. The bridesmaids were : —Miss Mice Biough (sister of the bridegroom), Mimes Barbara Grimsdale and Mildred Jackson (neices of the bride.) Miss Brough was attired in pale blue silk veiled with crepe-de-chine, embroidered with forget-me-nots, with lace yoke end sleeves, finished at the waist with pale blue silk girdle and Mewls. Her pale blue Tagel hat was wreathed with leaves and roses, with streamers of , palest blue satin ribbon, and she carried a handsome shower bouquet of pink roses. Her ornament was a gold pendant and chain, the gift of the bridegroom. The little bridesmaids wore dresses of white glactl silk veiled with ninon and trimmed with blonde lace and fichus, with white satin sashes. Their large Leghorn hats were wreathed with tiny pink roses and white strings. They carried posies of pick rosebuds and wore pendants and chains, the gifts of the bride- groom. Mr J. W. Beanland, of Liverp ,01, discharged the duties of best marl. The reception was held at Brentwood, about sixty guests attending. The catering was in the hands of the Winmarleigh Cato Co. Having received the hearty congratulations and good wishes of relatives and friends, Mr. and Mrs. Harry Brough left for St. liem and the Lake District where the honeymoon is being spent. The bride travelled in a smart tailor.made costume of navy blue serge, trimmed with silk braid, with Tagel hat to match. The following is a list of the presents:— .. . . . .

WHITECROSS WARD LIBERALS. Presentation to Councillor and Mrs. Hawthorn.

On Wednesday evming, at the tiands of the Whitecross Liberal Ward Committee. Councillor Joseph Hawthorn and Mrs. Hawthorn were the recipients of three handsome black and white pictures as a token of esteem in which the •former is held, and for the services which he has rendered to Liberalism. Prior to the presentation, at the invitation of Councillor and Mrs. Henry Roberts, the members of the Committee assembled in the Thewlis-strect schoolroom and partook of an excellent tea, after which some of their lady. friends joined them in order to witness the presentation and enjoy some capital musical items. Councillor Roberts presided. and on the platform were Councillor Hawthorn. Alderman Janice Evans. and Mr. Thomas Dale.

in; b;;;;;;an commented on the work of the Liberal representatives for Whit to and said that through their pertinacity they had secured the land near the Workhouse for the purpose of a school, and also for an open space. It was only hint to say that in this question Mr. fltllian had also been as energetic in bringiag that about as they had been. The provision of often spaces in populous distilicts were absolutely necessary, and though some objected that the land was too dear he would say that they could not Pay tan much for fresh air. There was a time when one felt inclined ta kick against the monoply of the land. Unfortunately they had to suffer front the fact that the whole of The land surrounding W rrr i r Eton belonged to a very few people. and once that desirable spot of land which they had just got had been allowed to go out of their possession, there would have been no further chance of obtaining it. They have had to pay through the nose for it, but it was one of the inevitable circumstances of thin country, until they had land courts like those in Ireland whereby the land would be assessed and reasonable arrangements would be made between buyers and sellers. (Applause.) After paying a great tribute to Mr. Hawthorn. Councillor Roberts w idled him every possible success and happiness in both his profession arid married life. He had made a good choice in the lady who had become his partner. and they wished him joy in everything that this world could give hint. (Applause.)

Alderman Evans, in making the presentation. said he had known Councillor Hawthorn for a great may years and he wax glad to think that there .cre such men as he in the Ward who were lightening the burdens ot such at he (the sneaker). In acknowledging the oretentation, Court. Hawthyn said he not only thanked them for the beautiful pictures but for the kindly sentiments which had accompanied them. fie heartily reciprocated and he would say_Ao those of them who were married he hoped their ataWied life had been as happy as his during the last two months: and to those who were unmarried he would advise them to take the Plunge as the (arm, would be dispelled after the first dip. (Laughter and apolapte.) Though at the last municipal election they won hand- somely they must out relax their efforts, for they had got a greet deal le do to maintain the reputation which the Ward had got. as the most Liberal Ward in the town. They had got to hold It for Liberalism because. after all, they had got a good cause to fight for. (Applause.) After referring to the progress of health matters in the town, the sneaker spoke of the land which the corporation had just bought. lie said the struggle had henna long one and the pay excessive, but they were in the hands of the landlord. They could not get it at less than what they were going to pay for it, though they did manage to get a little reduction from what the landlord originally wanted. But after all the ratepayers would be recouped. for the health of the distri:t would be improved. (Hear, hear.) In conclusion he observed that the Pictures which had been gi‘en to him would be a souvenir of a very interesting event in hit life, and he would always recollect that they had come from Whitecross Ward. ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

Queen of the Wicked."

It was a somewhat bold experiment to invent a further series of stage exploits for Sherlock Holmes, but in "Queen of the Wicked," which is presented for the first time in Warrington this week, Mr. Ronald Grahame has risen to the task with conspicuous success. His melodrama is one of the most powerful productions seen in Warrington for a considerable time. the action being so brisk and the many exciting incidents no cleverly conceived as to enchain the interest of the audience throughout the evening. "Queen of the wicked" is the unenviable title bestowed upon the beautiful but unscrupulous Ligeals, the moving spirit in a gang of desperadoes known as the" Great Murder Syndicate." Lord Trevelyan, the representative of an old and honoured English family, falls a victim to the subtle charms of the adventuress and becomes embroiled in the nefarious operations of the Syndicate. Naturally there is plenty of ecopo for Sherlock Holmes to display his phenomenal powers of deduction and resourcefulness, and he is always on the scene in the nick of time, ending be accomplishing one of the greatest coupe of his ca.retr. Mr. Grahame is to be congratulated upon the skill with which he has worked out the plot and woven a play of quite superior merit. Mr. and Mrs. Vernon Cowper and their company are also entitled to unstinted praise for the wholly admirable way in which the romance is etaged.First honours go to Miss Edith Blande, an experienced and talented actress., for her masterly portrayal of Ligeah. She never exagger- ates the part, but in always effective, the fascinating deception and cruel callousness of the character being brought out with an intense realism which occasion- ally arouses indignation among the " gods." Mr., Vernon Cowper scores a triumph as the manly yet irresponsible young peer, Lord Trevelyan, and deservedly wins the favour of the audience by his earnest and finished acting. Mr. Hy. W. Hatcheries is a calm, cool, and imperturbable Sherlock Holmes and lives up to the popular conception of this hero of fiction. Mims Eirene Douglas is a charming Lindy Doris Manners and lighter parts are capably sustained by Mrs Vernon Cowper as Belinda Brown and Mr Hy Egliuton as S,IaR

_ _ _ IRISH AFFAIRS. NOTES FOR IRISH READERS. - -

THE CONVENTION AT CORK.

The annual Convention of the United Irish league was held this year in the city of Cotk. A happier Ichoice could not have been antic. The welcome given ' to the exiles of Ireland was twice blessed, since it blessed the givers not less than the receivers For a, the procession which accompanied Mess's. T. P. O'Cont,r, M.P., and J. Devlin, Ml'., from the station, mated through the streets of Cork, the men sod women of the Southern Capita made manifesr to all Ireland that the real Cork was as solid and as strong in its adhesion to the national cause as ever—even though Factionist and Unionist have combined to capture two Parliamentary divisions. The Irishmen resident to irent Britain have every reason to be proud of the progress of their organisation and the splendid service it is rendering in the furthering of . Ireland's democratic aspirations. nit teen t ears ago the revenue of the United Irish League of Great Britain was e.l,e7se. Last year's figures totalled £5,326. The real worth f these figures and also the 1.:!.,655 which was subscribe' to the Parliamentary Fund, may be appreciated when it is borne in mind that in the main these sums are made up of small amounts contributed by Irish emigrants who are small wage earners, scattered through the factory and mining diet, iron of England, Scotland and Wales. Of the men and women who give so gener- ously, not. few of them have ever been privileged even to see this land of their constant devotion, and not • single one of them has any thought or posai- batty of material gain from any of the reforms in Ireland's condition which the past genera- tion has witnessed. It in doubtful whether in all Europe to-day you could find any attachment to a political organisatira so absolutely uo.lfieh and dismterestei. But sa many a Tory candidate and mummer in Great Britain has discovered in dismay that attachment to an ideal is not only impersonal and disinterested—it is wholly unpurchasable. Cork, therefore, honoured herself and her place in Irelaucl'e story in giving such • tremendous outburst of pastoral anti patriotic feeling—a demonstration quite unique in its enthusiasm according to seasoned campaigners— when these scattered exiles of Erin met within her bounds to conduct their yearly conference. The Agenda l'aper, a businesslike document, was gone through in a business like spirit, but the delegates did permit themselves the pleasnre of lustily cheering Captain Douelan when he came to join in their deliberations Mr. T. P. O'Connor took the chair as president for the 28th year of the organ i ear ion's existence. Always interesting,thia man who left his business and his task of earning his daily bread (at a time of life when most men of his pro- . feaeional position are secure and assured in their banking accounts) in order to travel thousands of miles in Canada and the States for funds to fight Unionist and ',scanting combinations, is never smite so attractive to the observant mind as when be pre- nines over gatherieg whose keen debates and earnest discussions skew what acuteness and agility in dialectics the Irish mind can diep,ay. You would imagine T.P. as he leans back in the chair, easy, not to say nonchalant, to Le quite indifferent and negligent of the storms that rage on some debated I point of the Constitution of the League or proposals fir its increased efficiency. But no point (escapes the man who ham piloted the ship through so vie difficult passages in the past and his occasional interventions in a conversational tone eliew how close is his grip on the most protracted diacussion. Mr. Joseph ' Devlin, M.P. was elected Vice-preeident and in the , election of representatives of the National Executive Mr. J. W. Dulanty again topped the list of the Lancashire representatives, Mr. Kenny, J.P., and Councillor A. Milord, being the other two gentlemen elected. THE COLONIAL PREMIERS AND • HOME RULE.

A gathering of more than ordinary significance was held the other day in the Rosa of Common.. It was the reception given by the Irish Party to the Colonial Premiers, presided over by Mr. John Redmond Attended by every Premier, the gathering, therefore, represented the whole of the Colonial Governments in the British Empire. Instead of epeechmaking, and in accordance with the invariable practice of the Irish Party on occasions of thin kind, the entertainment offered to the guests consisted of songs only, bet in a graceful little speech in which Sir Wilfrid Laurier thanked the artistes for their charming programme of Irish melodies, he made it clear that Ireland had a good friend in each 14 the distinguished statesmen who endorsed the policy of the Nationalist party. To the people who. talk so glibly and freely about Home Rule endanger- ing the security and unity of the Empire. and we suppose Sonia of Mr. Harold Smith's friends are of this etlioguided view, we commend the picture of every one of the Empire's premiers doing honour to the N'ationaliet party and its consistent demand for Ireland's self-government. " Let its think imperially " Was the great announcement of Mr. .Lnreph Chamber- lain a little while ago and that sentence is still claimed as one of the great principles of their creed, by Unionists to-day. We euggeat that this little Inaction which may not be blazoned forth in the Press, is • particularly instructive indication of Imperial thought with direct reference to the Irish (petition.

DEATH OF MR. T. H. SUTTON

Many of our reader% will regret to hear of the death of Mr. T. H. Sutton, superintendent registrar of War- rington, which occurred with painful !mildew:lean at hia residency, Wileon-Patten-atreet, on Sunday after- noon. The deceaaed, who was 48 yea, of one, had been in indifferent health for several weeke, but had attended to his duties as usual. So recently as Satur- day morning he waft at his office iii Bewsey-street, but on returning home he became seriously ill and passed away at five o'clock on Sunday, the cause of death being peritonitia and heart failure, lie had been attended hy Dr. Harold Joeeph, Mr. Sutton was the youngest son of Mr James Clowes Sutton, clerk to the Warrington Rural Metric', and for many years clerk to the Warrington Board of Guardians. He bad held the office of superintendent registrar for 'about twelve yearn and enjoyed the esteem of a large circle of hien& He leaves a widow and two sons. The interment took place at the Warrington Cemetery on Wednesday morning. the Rev. E. C. E. Oarleton officiating. The funeral, which wan strictly private, was only attended by relatives, exceming the Mayor (Councillor fir. Joseph) and Mr. Baron Joseph, who were the deceased gentleman's medical advisers. The coffin, which wan of oak and lined with lead, was covered with beautiful wreaths. Mr. h.F. Greenough was in charge of the funeral arrangemanta.

ST. HELENS. BAD LANGUAGE IN THE DOCK. James Moore,. young man having over twenty-one previous conviction+, was sent to gaol for sin months ! at St. Helens on Monday for drunken conduct and Assaulting Police-constahle Boden. Moore used bed 'swinge whilst in the dock, and in connerpience the! magistrntes imposed a severe parialty. SCHOOL PROCESSIONS. The great event at St. Helens on Whit Monday an. the Etonian Catholic primemion, which took place in the morning. About 12,000 children and members of guild. paraded the town, most of then] attired in pretty institutor's. The priests were met at the Town Hall by the Mayor (Alderman Beecham). In the afternoon the psrish Bible classes and schools of other churches held their field day in various parts of the district. MUNICIPAL ITEMS. Commitme !wee sent him a congrottilatory resolution.
